    'def.nilpotent': {
      statement: {
        en: "**What it does**: characterizes those operators that \"vanish after being applied repeatedly\". It is the protagonist of the structure theorem for nilpotent parts (everything in the Jordan form off the diagonal).\n\nLet $T : V \\to V$ be linear ($\\dim V = n < \\infty$). Call $T$ **nilpotent** if\n\n$T^{k} = 0$ (for some $k \\ge 1$)\n\nMatrix case: for $A^{k} = 0$, one calls $A$ a **nilpotent matrix**.\n\n**Equivalent conditions (often used as tests)**:\n\n1. all eigenvalues of $T$ are $0$;\n2. the characteristic polynomial is $x^{n}$;\n3. $T^{n} = 0$ ($n = \\dim V$ suffices — Cayley–Hamilton).\n\n**Structure**: a nilpotent operator can always be brought to a direct sum of Jordan blocks $J_{k}(0)$; the order of the largest block equals the **nilpotency index** of $T$ (the least exponent at which $T^{k} = 0$ first holds, namely $k$).",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: "v0.9.3#ad212464",
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**The standard example**: the shift operator $T(x_{1}, \\ldots, x_{n}) = (0, x_{1}, \\ldots, x_{n-1})$, with $T^{n} = 0$ while $T^{n-1} \\ne 0$. Its matrix is the \"shift up by one\" matrix, namely a single $J_{n}(0)$.\n\n⭐ **Why nilpotent operators matter so much**: every operator splits as \"diagonalizable part + nilpotent part\" $T = S + N$ ($S$ diagonalizable, $N$ nilpotent, $SN = NS$; this is the **Jordan–Chevalley decomposition**). So \"understanding nilpotent\" = \"understanding all the difficulty of non-diagonalizability\".\n\n⚠️ Nilpotent must **not** be confused with \"rank less than $n$\": $\\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\\\ 0 & 0\\end{pmatrix}$ is nilpotent, but $\\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 1\\end{pmatrix}$ has rank $1$ and is not nilpotent (its eigenvalues are $1$ and $0$).\n\nThe criterion is **all eigenvalues zero**, not rank.\n\n⚠️ A nilpotent matrix has trace and determinant both $0$, but **the converse fails** ($\\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\\\ 0 & -1\\end{pmatrix}$ has both equal to $0$ yet is not nilpotent).\n\n**Of the four criteria, \"$T^{n} = 0$\" looks most like a definition but is actually the most expensive**:\n\n- definition $T^{k} = 0$ (some $k$) $\\iff$ **all eigenvalues are $0$**: $(\\implies)$ is obvious ($0$ is the only possible eigenvalue); $(\\Leftrightarrow)$ needs the **characteristic polynomial to split** (over $\\mathbb{C}$ or an algebraic closure), otherwise the word \"eigenvalue\" is meaningless.\n- **characteristic polynomial $= x^{n}$** $\\iff$ all eigenvalues zero + adding algebraic multiplicities: this step uses **Vieta's formulas** and **requires finite dimension**.\n- **$T^{n} = 0$** ($n = \\dim V$): **this step uses Cayley–Hamilton** (thm.cayley-hamilton). ⚠️ It gives \"$k$ can be taken to be $n$\", whereas the definition only requires \"some $k$\" — this quantifier difference is exactly what Cayley–Hamilton provides.\n- **nilpotent $\\iff$ there is a basis in which the matrix is strictly upper triangular (with $0$ on the diagonal)**: this step uses the Jordan form or the splitting of the characteristic polynomial.\n\n⚠️ **Nilpotent $\\ne$ rank-deficient**: $\\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\\\0&1\\end{pmatrix}$ has rank $1 < 2$ but is not nilpotent (eigenvalue $1$). The criterion is always **all eigenvalues zero**.\n⚠️ **In infinite dimension \"$T^{n} = 0$\" fails**: the shift operator on $\\ell^{2}$ has no nonzero power equal to zero (though it can be \"locally nilpotent\") — so that criterion must explicitly say \"finite-dimensional\".",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: "v0.9.3#1c220bff",
      },

    'prop.discontinuity-types': {
      statement: {
        en: "Let $f$ be defined on a punctured neighbourhood of $x_0$ but **discontinuous** at $x_0$.         status: 'draft',
        basedOn: "v0.9.3#4ec39df9",
      },
      notes: {
        en: "⭐ This classification is not just terminology in the graph: **the discontinuities of a monotone function are all jumps, and there are at most countably many of them** — that is the starting point of the BV and measure-theory cluster (the atoms of $\\mu_F$, $F' = 0$ a.e.).\n\n⚠ The point of “removable” is that $f$ is **defined at $x_0$ but does not equal the limit**: for example $f(x) = \\sin x / x$ becomes continuous after filling in $f(0) = 1$.\n\nWhen $x_0$ is not in the domain at all (e.g. $1/x$ at $0$), it does not count as a discontinuity with domain $0$.\n\n⭐ The typical second-kind example: $\\sin(1/x)$ at $0$ (oscillation, the limit does not exist).\n\n**Proof** (the classification is exhaustive).\n\nAt a discontinuity $x_0$, each one-sided limit has only two states: exists and is finite, or does not exist / is infinite.\n\nThus “both one-sided limits are finite” and “at least one fails” are mutually exclusive and exhaustive, and the former splits according to whether the two limits are equal into removable and jump — the three cases are mutually exclusive and all possibilities are listed.\n\n∎",

      notes: {
        en: "⭐ **How much stronger it is than Liouville**: Liouville (`thm.liouville`) says “a bounded entire function is constant” and only rules out a bounded image; Picard says “the image cannot omit even two points” — even when the image is unbounded.\n\n**The proof given in this graph covers only the finite-order case** (this is an honest label): if $f$ has finite order and omits two values $a, b$, then $g := (f - a)/(f - b)$ is an entire function **omitting $0$ and $1$**.\n\nBy the Hadamard factorization (`thm.hadamard-factorization`), $g = e^{P}$;\n\nif $1$ is omitted, then $e^{P} - 1$ has no zeros $\\implies$ it also has a factorization $\\implies$; comparing growth orders successively forces $P$ to be constant, contradicting that $f$ is nonconstant.\n\n⚠️ **The general case requires Bloch's theorem or the modular function $\\lambda$** (the universal cover from the upper half-plane onto $\\mathbb{C}\\setminus\\{0,1\\}$); this graph has neither, so it is recorded as an **external input**.         en: "Let $f$ be an entire function.     'thm.poisson-formula': {
      statement: {
        en: "**In plain words**: the values inside the disc are **uniquely determined** by the values on the boundary, and by an explicit weighted average — the weight is the Poisson kernel, which “sharpens” near the boundary.\n\nLet $f$ be holomorphic on a neighbourhood of $B(0, R)$, $u = \\operatorname{Re} f$.         status: 'draft',
        basedOn: "v0.9.3#d5447a41",
      },
      notes: {
        en: "⭐ Use: to solve the **Dirichlet problem** on the disc — given a continuous boundary function $b$, replace $u(r e^{i\\theta})$ in the formula above by $b$; the resulting function is harmonic in the disc and tends to $b$ as one approaches the boundary value from inside.
